OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Introduction
Oakwood Ridge is a 558 home residential community proposed on 393 acres in the City of
Lake Wales. The property is the closed former Oakwood Golf Club, and it has been sitting
quiet for years while the neighborhoods around it have continued to live with the uncertainty
of what it will become next.
This document explains how the plan in front of you was arrived at. It is written in the order
the work actually happened. We met with the City before we drew anything. We read the 2040
Comprehensive Plan to understand what Lake Wales permits on this land and what it asks for.
We met with the neighboring residents. We met with major local employers and community
stakeholders. Only then did we start to lay out a plan.
The result is a community of 558 homes at a gross density of 1.42 dwelling units per acre, on
land where the adopted Future Land Use classification would allow up to 5. It preserves 193.4
acres (49.2% of the entire property) as open space. It introduces 53 executive homesites of a
size Lake Wales does not currently offer. And it repositions a closed golf course that is, at
present, going in one direction only.
THE PLAN AT A GLANCE
Site area	393.324 acres, 20 parcels
Residential units	558
Gross density	1.42 dwelling units per acre
Permitted by LDR	up to 5 dwelling units per acre
Open space	193.4 acres, 49.2% of the site
Natural preserved land	121.8 acres
Recreational trail	3.1 miles

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
The  property
The site sits on the Lake Wales Ridge. Ground elevations run from roughly 95 to 130 feet,
which is high ground by Florida standards and is where the second half of the project name
comes from. The first half comes from what is already growing here. The former fairways
roll. The oak canopy is mature and in many places continuous. There is open water, and there
are seven delineated wetlands running through the middle of the property, nearly all of
which is preserved in place.
Established single family neighborhoods adjoin the site on a few sides. Two pods of existing
homes sit inside the boundary of the former course and are thoughtfully considered in all
aspects of this project.
A closed golf course is one of the hardest properties in Florida to get right. It is large and
irregular in shape. It is surrounded on some sides by people who have looked at it out their
back windows for decades and who have every reason to care what happens next. It carries
real constraints that cannot be designed around casually. Handled badly, it becomes a fight
for the densest thing the land use map will bear, without consideration of others. Handled
well, it becomes the reason the neighborhoods around it are worth more than they were.
We  started	with  the  City
Before any lines were drawn, the Signature H team held a series of meetings with the City of
Lake Wales Planning Department to understand what the City wants from this property. We
learned the history of the property, both as a golf course and previous residential visions. We
asked "What is Lake Wales in need of and can this property provide it?". We also asked to be
pointed in the direction of residential board members, community stakeholders, and local
associations that may be willing to share their perspective on the property. This was the
beginning of our process, and we revisited the City throughout to further test our learnings.
We then explored the 2040 Comprehensive Plan closely to find out what the document
actually asks of a site like this one. The Low Density Residential classification, Policy I.1.2.12,
is specific. It is written to preserve the character of existing single family residential areas
with large lots. It permits up to 5 dwelling units per acre. And it expressly encourages the
clustering of housing units in order to preserve open space and natural resources.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Then	we  talked	to the neighbors
The next conversations were with the people who live here. We sat down with residents of the
abutting and interior communities and with HOA board members.
We went into those meetings with a straightforward position: these are neighbors, not
opposition. They have lived beside this property through its operating years and through its
closure, and they know things about it that no survey shows. Chief among them: with the
course closed and unattended, the property is used today mostly by trespassers running
trucks and ATVs across it, and that is what the neighbors live next to now.
We are not going to characterize those conversations as pure agreement, but we can say the
themes were consistent and that they are visible in the plan. Residents cared about the lot
sizes that would sit across from theirs. They cared about what would happen to the tree
canopy and the water. They cared about traffic and where it would enter and leave. They
cared, more than anything, about whether the property would be left in a state they could be
proud to live next to. Those concerns shaped the lot program, the conservation of land, and
the access strategy described below.
Then	we  tested	our  own	assumptions
Developers usually decide what to build and then look for evidence that they were right. We
tried to do it the other way around. Before settling on product type, the team met with
stakeholders and major employers in Lake Wales to pressure test the demand study.
I One phrase came back repeatedly and unprompted: executive housing.
Lake Wales does not currently have much of it. Employers recruiting professionals and
executives into the area feel that absence directly, because a candidate who cannot find the
house they want tends to buy it somewhere else and commute in, or not come at all. That is a
housing problem with an economic development consequence, and it is one this site is
unusually well suited to answer.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
What	that  led us  to this plan
Across Lake Wales, many of the recent residential approvals are medium density, small
footprint, and repetitive in product. That type has a place. This is simply not that, and it was
not designed to be.
Oakwood Ridge proposes 558 residential units on 393 acres, with 193 acres held for open
space. Gross density is 1.42 dwelling units per acre, or just over 2.0 when conservation areas
are excluded. Against a Future Land Use classification that permits up to 5.0 dwelling units
per acre, the plan uses under a third of the density available to it.
The residential program is 483 single family detached homes and 75 attached townhomes:
143 lots at	65 × 130 feet
287 lots at	80 × 130 feet
53 executive lots at	120 × 150 feet
75 attached townhome lots at 25 feet wide
Three commercial lots totaling 70,000 square feet of gross leasable area are planned within a
5.6 acre Neighborhood Activity Center at the northwest corner of the property, near Burns
Avenue. The lots are designed to support the community, providing everyday services close
to home for residents and the surrounding neighborhoods rather than drawing regional
traffic through it. All remaining land within the community is residential.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
The townhomes are designed in a traditional neighborhood pattern: masonry fronts,
articulated entries, individual stoops and walks to the street, and consistent street trees. They
are a real product for real buyers, including young professionals and residents downsizing
who want to stay in Lake Wales, and they are detailed to the standard of the rest of the
community rather than treated as the affordable corner of it.
Conceptual elevation, Traditional Neighborhood Design town homes, 25 foot lot widths.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
The  Executive	Home	Collection
The 53 executive homesites are the piece of this plan that does not currently exist anywhere
else in Lake Wales in purpose built form. Each lot is 120 feet by 150 feet, sized to carry a
residence of 3,500 to 5,000 square feet with the setbacks, side yard, and landscape frontage
that a home of that size requires in order to look right on its lot.
These homesites occupy some of the best ground on the property, with the best views it has
to offer, oriented to water and preserve frontage rather than to one another.
The case for them is simple. A city that wants to attract and keep employers needs
somewhere for the people those employers recruit to live. Right now that housing is bought
in Winter Haven, in Lakeland, or Orlando. Fifty three homesites will not by itself change the
regional market, but it establishes an address in Lake Wales that did not previously exist, and
it does so on land that already looks the part.
Conceptual view of the Executive Home Collection, 120 foot by 150 foot homesites for 3,500 to 5,000 square foot residences.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Compatibility	with  the  neighborhoods	around	it
This plan takes special consideration for the product type, buffer areas, and line of site from
the existing homes. The plan includes 65 foot and 80 foot lots to match the prevailing lot
widths of the existing Oakwood neighborhood. The 120 foot executive lots exceed the
prevailing widths, and the conservation and stormwater tracts create additional separation
along much of the shared boundary.
We would ask the City and the Board to consider the second half of that point as well. Meeting
the existing standard is the floor. A closed golf course does not bring value to the City nor the
homes that surround it. Reactivating this property with maintained streetscape, natural
preserves, community amenities, and a product range that includes homes larger than
anything currently built nearby raises the ceiling for the neighborhood rather than simply
avoiding harm to it.
South Entrance at Oakwood Ridge, detached homes beyond.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
A community,	not  a subdivision
We set out to build a community rather than a subdivision.
Oakwood Ridge preserves 193.4 acres, 49.2% of the entire property, as open space. That figure
breaks down into three parts:
121.8 acres
of natural preserved land, comprising 16.4 acres of preserved wetland and 105.5 acres of
upland preserve and buffer. The plan was laid out around the wetland system. Of 18.6 acres
delineated on the property, 16.4 acres are preserved in place and 2.2 acres are proposed for
impact, with mitigation permitted through the Southwest Florida Water Management District
and the US Army Corps of Engineers.
44.4 acres
of stormwater management tracts across 25 ponds, designed and shaped as water features
rather than as fenced back of house infrastructure. They are amenities that also happen to hold
water, and they are located where residents will see them.
27.2 acres
of parks and recreation areas in seven tracts. The City's adopted parks level of service standard
is 3.75 acres per 1,000 population. At this project size that standard would suggest 5.02 acres.
The plan provides 27.2 acres, more than five times what is required.
A 3.1 mile recreational trail threads the conservation areas and links the neighborhoods to
one another. It includes preserve overlooks with viewing platforms and direct connections to
the dog park and community parks, so that the trail functions as everyday circulation and not
only as recreation. The amenity program includes the renovated clubhouse, sport courts
with a pavilion, large and small dog parks, and multiple pocket parks distributed so that no
home is far from one.
The viewing platforms deserve a note of their own. The elevation change across this site,
from roughly 95 to 130 feet, produces long views that almost no residential property in
Florida can offer. From the high ground on the western side of the property, Bok Tower stands

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
on the horizon.
Conceptual view from the West Viewing Platform at The Preserve, overlooking conservation area and open water, with Bok
Tower on the horizon.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
The  clubhouse
The existing clubhouse is not being torn down. It stays, and the plan is to renovate it.
This came up often enough in conversations with residents that it deserves a direct
answer here. It is a good building, and it is the one structure on this property that carries
any history in Lake Wales. It deserves to be renovated, and it will be reopened as the
community clubhouse.
Conceptual view of the renovated clubhouse at Oakwood Ridge.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Drainage	and  stormwater	management
Stormwater management deserves a note of its own.
The site has real relief by Florida standards, running from roughly 95 feet to 130 feet, and its
existing drainage reflects that. Water moves toward low areas, wetlands, and surface water
features distributed across the property, and the site contains a mix of closed and open
basins. Existing water levels sit at roughly 97 to 100 feet in the northern portion of the
property and up to about 105 feet in the south. Portions of the property lie within FEMA Zone
A, the 100 year floodplain, as shown on the flood zone exhibit accompanying the
Development Impact Analysis.
The plan responds with 44.4 acres of stormwater management tracts across 25 ponds,
distributed through the community rather than pushed to its edges. Treatment will use off-
line retention sized to the first inch of runoff, or the first half inch for sub-basins smaller than
100 acres, with treatment volume recovered within 72 hours.
Final stormwater design is engineered and permitted through the Southwest Florida Water
Management District at a later stage of the approval process. Any system of this kind takes
specific engineering to permit. That is a normal step on the path from entitlement to
construction, and it is achievable on this site. The Districtʼs permit is the Cityʼs evidence of
drainage concurrency under Section 23-706 of the Land Development Regulations, and
drainage is a project level facility under the Cityʼs code, built by the developer rather than
drawn from existing City capacity.
Access,	utilities, schools,	and	fiscal contribution
Access is provided by two full-access driveways on Capps Road and one on CR 17A, Burns
Avenue. That distribution was chosen to spread trips across the existing network rather than
concentrate them at a single point, and it reflects what we heard from residents about where
traffic would and would not be welcome. A Traffic Impact Analysis prepared by Traffic
Planning and Design, Inc. accompanies this application and addresses operations in detail.
The property is served by connection to existing City of Lake Wales water and sewer mains
adjacent to the site.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Public education is provided by Polk County Public Schools, which is the authority for school
concurrency. Students generated by the community are assigned to Spook Hill Elementary
School and McLaughlin Academy of Excellence. The Lake Wales Charter Schools located
nearby are available to residents as schools of choice.
Estimated impact fees at buildout are approximately $13.54 million: $5.20 million payable to
the City of Lake Wales and $8.34 million payable to Polk County, the latter including
transportation and educational impact fees. Those figures are set out in full in the
accompanying Development Impact Analysis, and they are a floor rather than a forecast,
since scheduled rate increases will apply to most units. The community pays for the capacity
it uses, and 1,339 residents on 393 acres place a comparatively light load on that capacity for
the tax base they establish.

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
Since	the Planning	Board	hearing
The Planning and Zoning Board hearing was not the end of the conversation.
Since that hearing we have worked with City staff to compare notes and confirm that the
changes we made match what the Board asked for. We have held further meetings with
resident groups, and those meetings produced changes to the plan rather than reassurances
about it: new buffers, and the elimination of some homesites. Part of the reason the unit
count in this document is 558 rather than the 566 previously shown is that those
conversations took homes out of the plan.
Resident meetings will continue up to the City Commission hearing. The intent is that nobody
who lives beside this property hears something about it for the first time at a public hearing.
We are also preparing a landscape and tree preservation plan, work the Cityʼs planning staff
has encouraged, and we have asked Lake Wales Heritage to work alongside us on
preservation. Sections of the plan have already been removed to avoid disturbing trees. We
commit to preserving significant oaks and oak stands where possible, with particular
attention to sensitive areas and to the perimeter buffers adjoining existing homes, where the
existing canopy is the most effective screen available. Replacement planting will meet or
exceed the Cityʼs requirements.
Stewardship	and  timing
Buildout will be driven by absorption and could take up to ten years. That is a long horizon,
and it reflects both the scale of the property and the decision not to rush product to market at
the density the land use map would tolerate.
We intend to be a long term participant in Lake Wales. The natural preserved land, the trail
system, and the amenity program are not marketing that disappears after the last closing.
They are permanent features of the property that will be maintained by the community that
lives here.
Oakwood Ridge seeks no relief from City standards and one targeted map change, a 5.6 acre
Neighborhood Activity Center to serve the community. It proposes to use less than a third of
the density this land is entitled to, to preserve nearly half of it permanently, to build homes

OAKWOOD RIDGE	PROJECT NARRATIVE
that match what the neighbors already have and homes that are larger than anything near
them, and to reposition a closed golf course into productive use. We respectfully request the
City's approval, and we are available to City staff, the Planning and Zoning Board, and the
Commission for any question this document leaves open.
Respectfully submitted,
Helmut Wyzisk III
